The set of callers permitted to benefit from one another’s cached prefixes. Restricting reuse to it closes cross-domain prefix-cache timing channels, at the cost of fewer reuse opportunities.
shingle
A local feature used to compare records for near-duplicates, such as a short token sequence or a bounded subtree of parsed code.
source generation
A version identity for a source’s state, assigned under the source’s update contract and stronger than a filesystem modification timestamp. Derived index entries record the generation they came from, so current-state queries can reject entries from superseded generations.
source of record
The designated evidence source against which a particular claim is checked, such as a repository for a file’s contents at a revision or a recorded user statement for that user’s preference. The designation fixes where a claim is checked and confers no authority or permission.
span (tracing)
A record of one observed operation, with an identity, start and end boundaries, attributes, and optional timed events. Parent relationships express nesting, and links connect asynchronous work that does not fit one nested lifetime.
specification boundary
The distinction between proposing a task contract and adopting it as binding. Models can assist requirements, designs, and tests; authorized principals adopt goals, grants, acceptance criteria, and revisions, and own the residual duties after acceptance.
speculative decoding
A serving method that uses draft proposals and a target-model acceptance rule. Distribution-preserving variants retain the target distribution under their algorithmic assumptions; net latency and cost depend on accepted prefixes, verification work, batching, and load.
spending alarm
A retrospective monitor on the charge rate over a trailing window. It can trigger investigation or a hold on new work, but the spending it measures has already happened.
split leakage
Information about an evaluation target reaching training through a related record, such as another solution to the same underlying task placed on the training side of a split.
stale-policy window
The interval, under cached authorization, in which a newly revoked grant may still be accepted. Its bound must cover cache age, renewal, expiry, and clock assumptions and must hold through a partition.
stale-return rate
The fraction of evaluated current-state queries that return any record made inapplicable by a relevant source change, with historical queries reported separately. An empty response scores zero, so the rate is always reported beside evidence coverage.
stale-support rate
The fraction of decisions requiring current evidence that treat superseded support as current. A historical reference to an older state is not an error; using it to assert a property of the current state without support is.
standby reserve
A pool of clean, unassigned execution instances, also called a warm pool. Admission consumes one and binds it to a task; provisioning replenishes it, and an ended lease rejoins only after its reset contract is satisfied.
state-delta verification
Checking specified properties of an observed change between declared initial and resulting environment states, using measurements tied to the relevant versions and task criteria. It distinguishes a claimed repair from an observed change that meets the selected checks.
stateless cursor
A pagination token that carries its own position, such as a query digest, last key returned, and issue time, together with a message authentication code only the tool can produce. Abandoning it costs the server nothing, at the price of reading current rather than snapshot data and a longer token.
static plan
A plan that declares its possible stages and dependencies, including conditional branches and bounded retries, before execution, so its handoffs are inspectable and a worst-case resource bound can follow.
static reservation
A KV allocation policy that reserves a contiguous buffer for the configured token cap when a request is admitted. The request grows without reallocating, but the unused remainder stays unavailable to every other request for as long as the reservation lives.
stochastic computer
The single map, drawn once in the introduction, that relates the parts of an agentic system to the parts of a computer: the model to a processor, context and stores to memory, tools to input and output, the runtime to an operating system. It is a map for orientation, not a mechanism.
stop reason
The service’s report, retained in a model response, of why generation yielded control, such as a finished turn, emitted tool calls, the output limit, or a stop sequence. The runtime keeps it as raw evidence alongside the normalized status it derives.
storage invalidation
The exclusion of a derived record, such as an index entry, vector, summary, or cached result, from current-state retrieval once a relevant change to its source makes it inapplicable, independent of when a replacement is built.
streaming
Delivery of response content before generation finishes. A delivery chunk may carry part of a token, several tokens, or a protocol event, so a runtime follows the service’s framing contract rather than equating chunks with tokens.
streaming cancellation
A harness request that asks the serving service to stop generating a reply in progress, closes the invocation’s eligibility to produce an actionable candidate, and retains partial content only for bounded diagnosis. A canceled or truncated payload cannot become a dispatchable call.
structural retrieval
Retrieval over program structure rather than text, distinguishing an enclosing function, a declaration, and an identifier used in an expression. A parser supplies the syntax tree and source spans, and symbol resolution or further analysis adds relationships between definitions and references.
structured progress record
A continuation record that preserves a trajectory’s state in named fields, such as current task requirements, relevant observations, hypotheses, artifact identities, and unfinished work, so the next call can continue after the detailed history leaves the prompt. It is not an execution checkpoint.
subgraph resynthesis
A revision tier that replaces an artifact and all its descendants, asking the model to bridge from the still-supported frontier to the downstream requirements that remain unchanged.
supersession
Replacing a stored record with a new version for the same subject and scope while marking the old version superseded, which ends its eligibility for current decisions but keeps it as history.
supervised fine-tuning (SFT)
Adjusting trainable model parameters to increase the likelihood of selected target tokens under their supplied prefixes. Training an agent this way inherits both a target-selection contract and a conditioning contract.
supervision tree
The structure that assigns responsibility for child lifetimes and outstanding work among agents.
supervisor-worker topology
A coordination arrangement, also called orchestrator-worker, in which one coordinator decomposes the task, dispatches children, and integrates their receipts.
system-call tax
The per-call cost an isolation envelope adds to a mediated system call, up to three terms: crossing a privilege boundary, checking or emulating the request, and handing control to another thread or process.

T

tail sampling
Choosing which detailed traces to retain using evidence accumulated later, such as a failed acceptance check or an unusually costly run. It concentrates diagnostic storage on important cases, so the retained collection is deliberately unrepresentative of ordinary traffic.
tail waste
The uninitialized slots in a sequence’s final KV block. With \(\KvBlockSize\) tokens per block it ranges from zero to \(\KvBlockSize-1\), and a length divisible by \(\KvBlockSize\) wastes nothing.
taint tracking
Runtime tracking of data origins and dependencies through transformations, used to enforce policy at later uses. Integrity provenance and confidentiality answer different questions; through a model, conservative propagation records a policy assumption rather than proven semantic causation.
task account
The account of an initiated task that includes all its attempts and delegated branches once, plus its share of capacity and operations costs. Failed or canceled branches stay in the account after their output is discarded.
task contract
The five-part specification of a delegated task: its goal with negative scope, the environment, the permitted actions, the available observations, and the completion criteria at a stated evidence level.
task fixture
A versioned package of the initial state and dependencies needed to instantiate a task, such as a starting repository, dependency versions, service behavior, and permitted data. It sets the conditions of an attempt; separate acceptance criteria judge the result.
task group
The repeated attempts, paraphrases, derived fixtures, and solution-equivalent variants of one underlying problem, grouped so they are treated as one task. The group records the basis on which its members were judged equivalent.
teacher forcing
Predicting each selected token from the recorded preceding tokens, including earlier selected responses, rather than from a freshly sampled continuation.
temperature
A positive sampling parameter that divides the logits before softmax. Lower temperature concentrates probability on higher-scoring tokens and higher temperature flattens the distribution; it carries no information about whether an answer is correct.
temporal-difference residual
The sampled one-step quantity \(\TemporalDifferenceResidual_k = \TurnReward_k + \PolicyRemainingReturn(\InteractionHistory_{k+1}) - \PolicyRemainingReturn(\InteractionHistory_k)\), whose conditional expectation equals the advantage when the value function is exact. One residual is a single noisy sample, and estimated values add further error.
test-tampering defense
Controls protecting designated acceptance logic and evidence from modification or interference by the agent and its submitted artifacts. Read-only test files are one control, not a complete grader-protection boundary.
test-time compute
Inference computation spent on a single decision beyond one model call, through longer reasoning, additional candidates, or check-and-revise rounds, before the runtime admits a result.
test-time compute budget
The per-decision allowance of processed and generated tokens, model calls, checks, and elapsed time that a runtime may spend, with the model’s weights fixed, before it must select a candidate, abstain, or escalate. An exhausted budget is not evidence that the best remaining candidate is acceptable.
test-time deliberation rollout
One bounded candidate-generation path within a decision, possibly including checks and revisions conditioned on observations, produced with the model’s weights fixed.
threat model
A statement of what the attacker controls, what must remain protected, how the controlled material enters the task, and what outcome counts as a violation. A list of attack names fixes neither the boundary nor the success criterion.
time per output token (TPOT)
The average interval between generated tokens after the first in a streamed model response.
time to first token (TTFT)
The client-observed interval from sending a model request to receiving its first generated token. It includes queueing and transport as well as the prefill pass that produces that token’s distribution.
time-to-live (TTL)
A policy that sets an age after which the runtime requests a refresh of an observation. It is a refresh heuristic chosen for the task’s change rate and cost of error, not a validity test, since a younger observation may already be outdated and an older one may still be current.
time-travel replay
Reconstructing supported recorded state at a retained historical boundary from compatible checkpoints, history, and required artifacts. Its scope depends on capture and retention; it does not recreate arbitrary past external-world state.
token averaging
A loss normalization that assigns equal coefficients to every selected target token’s loss. Context and padding tokens are never counted.
token budget
The per-iteration limit on token positions a chunked-prefill scheduler admits, where each decode request contributes one position and each prefill chunk contributes its length. It bounds work per iteration, not time, so it must be calibrated against measured gaps between output tokens.
token entropy
The entropy of a specified next-token distribution at a delivered prefix, measuring uncertainty over the vocabulary. Reports must state whether it is the model’s distribution or the sampler’s after temperature, filtering, and constraints.
tokenizer
A component that maps text into identifiers from a finite vocabulary. A token may be a word, part of a word, whitespace, punctuation, or a combination, so token boundaries need not match the boundaries of an identifier or a JSON field.
tombstone
A durable exclusion marker that suppresses retrieval of a record, or of every record from a source generation, before physical cleanup finishes. It records ineligibility, not erasure.
tool
An interface to an operation in the environment, such as reading a file, applying a patch, or running a test. The model can only propose a tool call; the runtime decides whether it is dispatched.
tool-choice control
A serving-interface control that constrains whether the model proposes a tool call at all: it may allow an optional call, require a call, require one named tool, or forbid calls. Forcing a call shapes the proposal but authorizes nothing.
tool descriptor
The description of a tool operation that communicates its purpose, arguments, result, and relevant effect semantics. It helps the model choose and parameterize the operation; the runtime and endpoint enforce the contract.
tool-wait problem
Retained serving state held idle while a slow tool runs, forcing a choice between keeping, moving, or dropping that state before the next model call.
top-\(\TopTokenSampleCount\) sampling
A sampling restriction that keeps the \(\TopTokenSampleCount\) highest-scoring tokens, with a defined tie rule, and renormalizes their probabilities.
top-\(\NucleusProbabilityMass\) sampling
A sampling restriction, also called nucleus sampling, that keeps the shortest set of most probable tokens whose cumulative probability reaches a threshold \(\NucleusProbabilityMass\) and renormalizes it. Unlike top-\(\TopTokenSampleCount\), the number of tokens kept varies with the shape of the distribution.
torn dependency read
A hazard in which a worker observes an intermediate mixture of state, such as a changed interface whose callers are not yet updated, that no worker proposed.
trace
A graph of spans recording one trajectory’s model calls, tool calls, approvals, and child agents, with their durations, token counts, and outcomes, used to measure and attribute behavior rather than to recover it.
trajectory
The ordered sequence of recorded states, proposed actions, runtime decisions, and observations through which one delegated task proceeds, from its first context to its acceptance, stop, or escalation. It is the unit of engineering in agentic systems.
trajectory curation
Selecting, labeling, and transforming recorded interactions for a declared learning use while preserving the evidence and constraints that justify that use. Its output is a versioned artifact with specified consumers, not a collection of success labels.
trajectory goodput
Distinct task completions accepted under a declared contract per unit of elapsed observation time. Goodput per dollar instead divides accepted tasks by the full included cost, retaining failed and canceled work; it is the reciprocal of cost per accepted task when the relevant denominators are positive.
trajectory-level sticky routing
Assigning a configuration at the task or trajectory boundary and preserving it through continuation, retries, and resumed work, so one measured task never mixes baseline and candidate behavior. It is a stable configuration assignment, not a promise to keep one physical model instance.
trajectory log
The append-only, ordered sequence of typed events (delivered model inputs, returned replies, admissions, control changes, and observed tool outcomes) from which a deterministic reducer rebuilds a trajectory’s control state. Recorded claims are not automatically external truth.
trajectory record
The per-trajectory control state that the harness owns and the model cannot write directly: identity, lifecycle state and version, per-call limits, actual and reserved consumption, grants and leases, observation eligibility, unresolved effect obligations, and references to context, live plan, log, and workspace.
trajectory saga
A workflow that records its constituent actions and coordinates compensating actions when their established effects must be remediated, while preserving unresolved outcomes and dependencies. Compensation is registered before forward dispatch and follows dependencies and outcome evidence.
transaction isolation
The transaction property that specifies which concurrent observations and interleavings are permitted. Serializable isolation has the effect of some serial order but need not preserve real-time order.
trusted computing base
The set of components whose correct operation a claimed protection depends on. Changing the isolation envelope changes that set and the interfaces exposed to untrusted code.
turn
One step of a trajectory: one model call and the runtime’s handling of the action it proposes. The runtime’s record marks whether each proposed action was rejected, dispatched, or completed.
turn boundary
A local admission boundary before a model invocation or tool dispatch at which the harness checks whether continuation remains permitted. Reaching this boundary does not establish that earlier remote effects have ended.
two-phase commit
A protocol that coordinates one atomic commit decision among participating resource managers: each participant prepares and preserves recovery state, and a participant that votes to commit cannot then abort unilaterally. A prepared participant may block while it cannot learn the decision.
typed task envelope
A versioned child assignment binding task identity, input references, scoped authority, resource limits, completion requirements, and a bounded return format. The runtime enforces those bindings; schema validity alone establishes neither authority nor successful completion.

U

uncertain outcome
The recorded status of a dispatched operation whose effect the runtime can neither confirm nor exclude, because its reply was lost, late, or never produced. It stays unsettled until an endpoint deduplication contract, a reconciliation query, or an authorized decision establishes what happened.
universal scalability law (USL)
Gunther’s model of relative capacity for a fixed workload divided among \(\WorkerCount\) workers, \(\WorkerCount/(1+\ContentionCoefficient(\WorkerCount-1)+\CoherencyCoefficient \WorkerCount(\WorkerCount-1))\), where \(\ContentionCoefficient\) is the contention (serialized share) and \(\CoherencyCoefficient\) the coherency (pairwise reconciliation) cost. Nonzero coherency gives it a peak beyond which adding workers lowers capacity.
untrusted span
Text staged in a request whose origin the runtime can record but whose content it did not author and cannot vouch for. Encoding keeps the span in its field, yet the model may still follow an instruction it contains. See also indirect prompt injection.
usable working context
The context content, organization, and length with which a specified model and invocation configuration meet a chosen quality criterion on a stated workload. It is established only by evaluation and is usually much shorter than the nominal context capacity. See also nominal context capacity.
user-space kernel
An isolation envelope that interposes another implementation of the application’s operating-system interface, such as gVisor’s Sentry, in front of the host kernel. Applications depend on the interfaces it supports, and exposed resources still need their own constraints.

V

verification gap
The distance between the probability that at least one of \(\IndependentAttemptCount\) runs passes (pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\)) and the probability that all \(\IndependentAttemptCount\) pass (pass\(^\IndependentAttemptCount\)). A selector with a reliable checker can turn availability into one accepted delivery, but work that must pass every time it recurs gets only consistency.
verifier
A check whose criteria and observations the candidate under assessment cannot modify. Its role in acceptance depends on the task’s evidence contract, and passing one verifier is narrower than task acceptance.
verifier cascade
An ordered sequence of declared predicates that routes a recorded trajectory to a learning artifact, stopping that route at the first predicate that does not pass. Predicate order changes checking cost without changing the accepted set.
verifier-guided selection
A selection policy that returns a candidate only when a verifier, a check whose criteria and observations the candidate cannot modify, passes it, and abstains when no candidate passes.
verifier isolation
Protection of an assessment from both the running agent and the code the agent submits, so the agent cannot touch the verdict. Isolation settles who can alter the verdict, not whether the checks cover the intended task.
visible test
A test whose cases the agent can inspect. Visible tests are useful for diagnosis but also expose the examples a candidate can be fitted to. See also sealed test.
vocabulary mismatch
The problem that people describe the same thing with different words, so a query phrased by symptom may share no terms with the source that answers it. Identifier splitting, synonym expansion, query reformulation, or dense retrieval can recover some matches.

W

wait policy
The declared rule stating which tool results end a trajectory’s wait. The return to a ready state passes through a guarded transition that evaluates the policy again.
whole-trajectory cost
The total cost attributed to one execution attempt across its model calls, environment, assessment, and required finalization within a declared accounting boundary. Cost per accepted task aggregates it over a cohort’s attempts and divides by the distinct accepted tasks.
Wilson score interval
A confidence interval for a binomial rate that uses the hypothesized rate rather than the observed one in the variance. Unlike the Wald interval, it neither collapses to zero width when every outcome agrees nor extends below zero near the boundaries.
working-set decision
The runtime’s choice of which source excerpts, observations, constraints, and unresolved questions belong in the next model request, selecting the information that supports the next bounded step.
workspace lease
A binding of a workspace to its task, owner, permitted operations, and lifetime. It lets edits and selected caches persist across turns without making them available to the next tenant.
write-ahead logging
Durably recording an admitted request, with its authorized intent and admission state, before dispatching it. The durable history then identifies an operation that might have executed even if its reply never reaches the worker; inferring that a missing intent was never dispatched additionally requires the completeness rule.
write amplification
Bytes written per byte of new state. For full snapshots it arises from serializing unchanged state again at every checkpoint, so it grows with trajectory length, while delta checkpoints avoid it.

Z

zero ambient authority
The property that the model itself holds no rights to read, change, or reach anything; every effect it proposes takes place only through a grant and a capability the runtime issues and enforces.
zero-contrast group
A completed group of rollouts whose rewards are all equal, so every group-relative advantage is zero and the reward term supplies no gradient. Equal rewards say nothing about how diverse the attempts were.
Back to top
